L288: Phase 1
L289: A total of 361 subjects, 317 of whom received lomitapide, participated in 14 phase 1 clinical trials, spanning 1996-2011. These trials are summarized in Table 7, Table 8, and Table 9. Five phase 1 studies included multiple daily doses of oral lomitapide:
L290: • CV145-002 (six subjects per cohort of 10 mg, 25 mg, 50 mg, or 100 mg daily x 14 days)
• CV145-010 (six women per cohort of 10 mg or 25 mg daily x 14 days)
• AEGR-733-013 (16 subjects in warfarin DDI study received 60 mg daily x 12 days)
• AEGR-733-015 (27 subjects in Ortho CycLEN® DDI study received 50 mg daily x 8 days)
• AEGR-733-019 (16 subjects in simvastatin DDI study received 60 mg daily x 7 days)
L291: Subjects in the remaining phase 1 studies either received a single dose or more than one single dose separated in time (e.g., six single-dose treatment sequences in the food-effect study CV145-005).
L292: Phase 2 Program (excluding HoFH-pilot)
L293: A total of 755 subjects, 579 of whom received lomitapide, participated in 7 phase 2 clinical trials, spanning 1999-2008. These trials included AEGR-733-002 (a drug-drug interaction study briefly described in Section 7.5.5, p. 242), the six-subject HoFH-pilot, and the five phase 2 studies listed in Table 6, which were conducted in hyperlipidemic non-HoFH subjects.
L294: Because there is only one 29-subject phase 3 trial, each of the five non-HoFH phase 2 studies proposed to support efficacy were reviewed individually. In this section, I will review the major design features and results from each of these five trials. The trial designs are described in greater detail in the Appendix (p. 258).
L295: Study CV145-009
L296: Study CV145-009 (“C-009”) was conducted by BMS in 1999 to compare the effects of lomitapide 25 mg daily x 4 weeks with matched placebo on the reversibility of hepatic fat content as measured by MRI/NMRS in healthy subjects with hypercholesterolemia (fasting LDL-C \geq 160 mg/dL and TG \leq 500 mg/dL, obtained off lipid-lowering agents after a lipid-stabilization period). Following a 4-week lipid stabilization period and institution of a NCEP Step 1 diet (\leq 30\% of total calories from fat) and a 4-week placebo lead-in during which qualifying LDL-C values were obtained, 76 subjects were randomly assigned to lomitapide 25 mg daily or matched placebo for 4 weeks followed by a 6-week off-treatment period.
L297: C-009 Demographics &amp; Disposition
L298: The lomitapide and placebo arms were balanced well with respect to demographic and baseline characteristics. Of the 76 subjects enrolled, 48 (63%) were men; 69 (91%) were white, 6 (8%) were black, and 1 (1%) was Asian/Pacific islander. The mean (SD) age was 52 (10) years with a range of 24 to 65 years. The mean (SD) BMI was 28.0 (4.1) kg/m2. Mean LDL-C at baseline was 190 mg/dL.
L299: Table 11 summarizes the disposition of subjects in study C-009. There was a substantially higher drop-out rate in the lomitapide group (31.6%) compared with the placebo group (2.6%).

L304: The “other” reasons for discontinuation in the BMS-201038 (lomitapide) group were (1) not having the baseline MRI archived (Subject 002-001, who took drug for 3 days before discontinuation), and (2) increased transaminases (not reported as AE) (subject 002-022, who took drug for 20 days before discontinuation). The only subject in the placebo
L305: group who discontinued early was because of a conflict between the subject's personal schedule and the end-of-study visit.
L306: C-009 Efficacy Results
L307: Table 12 summarizes the baseline values and % changes in lipid parameters after 4 weeks of treatment (lomitapide 25 mg daily or matched placebo) and 6 weeks after drug discontinuation (weeks B5 and B11, respectively). The placebo-subtracted % change in LDL-C from baseline to week 4 was -57.7% among completers (P &lt; 0.0001). After six weeks off-treatment, LDL-C had largely returned to baseline (-5.8% placebo-subtracted change from baseline). Note that these values only include subjects with available data (i.e., no imputation). The longitudinal profiles of these parameters, expressed as placebo-subtracted % change from baseline, are depicted in the subsequent figure.

L324: Adverse Events
L325: There were no SAEs or deaths in this study. Treatment-emergent AEs (TEAEs) occurred in 35 (92%) and 17 (45%) of the lomitapide and placebo groups, respectively (Table 14). The AE profile was qualitatively similar to that observed among subjects with HoFH, i.e., primarily dominated by gastrointestinal events (Table 15). The AEs contributing to premature discontinuation of lomitapide included at least one gastrointestinal AE in 8 (21%) subjects (diarrhea [n=7]; nausea/vomiting [n=4]);
L326: abdominal pain [n=2]; and decreased appetite, distension abdomen, eructation, and flatulence [n=1 each]), general AE in 3 (8%) subjects (fatigue [n=2]; malaise, volume depletion, weakness, and weight loss [n=1 each]), hepatic/biliary AE in 3 (8%) subjects (liver function test increase [n=2]; ALAT increased, ASAT increased, and hepatomegaly [n=1 each]); and nervous system AE in 1 (3%) (headache). No subjects assigned to placebo discontinued prematurely because of an AE.

L335: Only TEAEs that were more common in the lomitapide than placebo group and were reported by ≥5% of subjects (i.e., ≥2 subjects) in the lomitapide group are listed.
L336: Although adverse event severity assessments are subjective, among the 35 subjects assigned to lomitapide who experienced a TEAE, 25 (71%) reported that the event was
L337: either moderate (n=17) or severe (n=8) in intensity. Among the 17 subjects assigned to placebo who experienced a TEAE, 6 (35%) reported that the event was moderate intensity and none reported severe intensity.
L338: As shown in Table 15, diarrhea was the most frequently reported TEAE. Among lomitapide-treated subjects, the severity of diarrhea was mild in 8 (26%) of the 31 who reported this symptom, moderate in 16 (52%), and severe in 7 (23%). Often, the diarrhea was accompanied by nausea/vomiting, abdominal pain, and/or abdominal distension.
L339: Liver-related Laboratory Values
L340: In C-009, 4 (11%) of 38 lomitapide-treated subjects discontinued because of abnormal transaminases. The mean absolute change from baseline in ALT increased during lomitapide treatment at weeks B2, B3, B4, and B5 (+9.09, 30.84, 51.90, and 62.3 U/L, respectively), but decreased 6 weeks after drug discontinuation at week B11 (+28.74 U/L compared with baseline). The placebo mean absolute changes from baseline were minor (-0.14, -0.11, +0.97, 1.11, and 4.05 U/L). AST tracked with ALT but changes were of lower magnitude.
L341: Based on my analysis of the submitted C-009 laboratory dataset, 12 (32%) subjects assigned to lomitapide and no subjects assigned to placebo had a peak ALT \geq 3\times ULN (Table 16). Peak transaminase values typically occurred after drug discontinuation, in three cases approximately 2 weeks later, but this was generally followed by resolution. One subject, however, was followed out to day 451 with continued elevation in transaminases. This subject also had a value above the normal range at baseline (60 U/L; 1.3x ULN). Longitudinal profiles of ALT for subjects who ever experienced ALT \geq 3\times ULN are shown in Figure 4.

L349: Hepatic Fat
L350: In C-009, each subject had MRI/NMRS performed at baseline, after 4 weeks of treatment (lomitapide 25 mg daily or matched placebo), and 6 weeks after drug discontinuation (weeks A4, B5, and B11, respectively). If the % hepatic fat content at the end-of-study visit did not decrease by a pre-specified amount, which depended on the baseline and end-of-treatment values, a fourth NMRS was performed 12 weeks after drug discontinuation (week B17).
L351: Subjects treated with lomitapide 25 mg daily for 4 weeks had a mean 20.88% (absolute percentage points) increase in % hepatic fat from baseline to the end of treatment; those treated with placebo had a 0.56% increase, yielding a difference of 20.32% (95% CI 17.08-23.56; p &lt; 0.0001). Six weeks after discontinuation, subjects treated with lomitapide had a mean 4.02% hepatic fat, which was not statistically significantly different than placebo (mean difference between groups 3.11% [95% CI, -0.98 to 7.20; p = 0.13]) (Table 17).

L367: * 001-00003 discontinued after 18 days of lomitapide because of increased ALT, AST, and hepatomegaly. The ALT peaked at 643 IU/L (13.7x ULN) on day 33, approximately 2 weeks after discontinuing drug. It fell to 387 IU/L at day 45 and ultimately to 46 IU/L on day 108.
L368: ** 003-00012 discontinued after 21 days of lomitapide because of decreased appetite, diarrhea, fatigue, and weight loss, but this subject also had increased ALT and AST. The ALT was 141 IU/L (2.5x ULN) at day 22 and peaked at 173 IU/L (3.1x ULN) on day 35. It appears the subject was followed for at least 351 days, with an ALT of 64 IU/L at the time.
L369: Reviewer Comment: Lomitapide-induced increases in hepatic fat appear largely reversible (although not universally) within weeks, at least after short-term (4-week) use.
L370: I explored the association between baseline % hepatic fat and peak ALT observed on study among lomitapide-treated subjects only. Among the 8 lomitapide-treated subjects with baseline hepatic fat &gt;15%, 6 had a peak ALT \geq 3\times ULN, 1 had a peak ALT 2.5\times ULN, and 1 remained within the normal range throughout. Simple linear regression did not suggest a statistically significant relationship between these variables (p=0.10), although the sample size is small. The point estimate for the slope was positive, however, which would be consistent with higher % hepatic fat at baseline being associated with higher peak transaminase values, on average (Figure 5).

L374: Last, I explored the relationship between peak ALT during the study, expressed as multiples above the ULN, with absolute % change in hepatic fat (from baseline to end-of-treatment). There was a statistically significant association between these observations among lomitapide-treated subjects, with the peak ALT observed explaining nearly the 20% of the variation in the change in hepatic fat (p=0.02) (Figure 6). The association between peak ALT expressed relative to baseline ALT (i.e., on the day of study drug initiation for this analysis) and change in hepatic fat was similar (not shown).

L379: Reviewer Comment: These exploratory analyses do not establish a causal relationship between hepatic fat and the changes in transaminases observed. Clinically, however, there would be greater concern for increases in hepatic fat associated with ALT elevations.
L380: Vitamin E
L381: Mean levels of total vitamin E decreased during lomitapide treatment (-49.5% placebo-subtracted; P &lt; 0.0001) but returned to near-baseline levels 6 weeks after drug discontinuation. This was largely the result of a reduction in plasma lipids, which carry vitamin E; between-group differences in changes in the vitamin E:lipid (TC+TG) ratio, however, were not detected (-0.3% placebo-subtracted; P = 0.49) (Table 20). Other fat-soluble nutrients were not assessed.

L385: Laboratory Results
L386: Measures of central tendency of hematology, serum chemistry, urinalysis, and coagulation test results did not suggest clinically significant differences between lomitapide and placebo with the exception of ALT and AST, which are discussed elsewhere. The relevant data in the C-009 CSR are Tables 14.7.1, *.2, *.4, *.5, and *.8.
L387: Vital Signs
L388: Mean systolic blood pressure, diastolic blood pressure, and pulse were similar between subjects treated with lomitapide and placebo. After four weeks of treatment (Week B5), the between-group difference (lomitapide – placebo) in mean SBP was -3.1 mmHg, in mean DBP was -1.6 mmHg, and in mean pulse was +0.1 bpm. Additional data are summarized in the C-009 CSR in Table 14.8.1.
L389: Weight Change
L390: Among completers, the mean change in weight from baseline was -1.9% in the lomitapide group and +0.1% in the placebo group (P&lt;0.0001), a between-group difference of -2.1% change from baseline (95% CI, -2.9 to -1.2; P&lt;0.0001).

L398: Reviewer Comments:
L399: 1. Subjects who may be at greater risk of developing hepatic steatosis (diabetes, alcohol consumption) were excluded from this study.
2. Protocol amendment #3 added a discontinuation criterion for ALT &gt;3x ULN as a result of 4 (10%) of 41 subjects randomized at the time having been discontinued because of abnormal transaminases as high as 643 IU/L. This may have reduced the incidence of transaminase abnormalities of greater magnitude.
3. Given the rather large proportion of missing data in the lomitapide group (26%) and the fact that these data are likely not missing at random, these results may be biased. It is plausible that the missing subjects may have had larger amounts of hepatic fat at follow-up or may have been at higher risk for other adverse effects.
4. Lomitapide-induced increases in hepatic fat appear largely reversible (although not universally) within weeks, at least after short-term (4-week) use.
L400: Study AEGIR-733-001
L401: Because dosages of lomitapide 25-100 mg/day in previous phase 1 and 2 studies were associated with hepatic and gastrointestinal adverse events, Aegerion sought to determine whether dosages lower than 25 mg/day could provide clinically significant
L402: reductions in LDL-C with an improved AE profile. Study AEG-733-001 (“A-001”) was conducted in 2006 to evaluate whether 12 weeks of lomitapide in combination with ezetimibe 10 mg daily was superior to monotherapy with either agent in adult subjects with fasting LDL-C \geq 160 mg/dL (0-1 NCEP risk factors) or \geq 130 mg/dL (\geq 2 NCEP risk factors or prior CHD), obtained after a 4-week washout period of lipid-lowering agents. Lomitapide was force-titrated from 5 mg to 7.5 mg to 10 mg daily at 4-week intervals. Subjects were counseled to implement a low-fat diet (&lt;20\% calories from fat).
L403: A-001 Demographics &amp; Disposition
L404: Of the 85 subjects enrolled, 40 (47%) were men; 62 (73%) were white, 22 (26%) were black, and 1 (1%) was Hispanic. The mean (SD) age was 55 (7) years with a range of 37 to 71 years. The mean (SD) BMI was 29.2 (6.0) kg/m2. Mean (SD) LDL-C at baseline was 167 (28) mg/dL with a range of 113 to 260 mg/dL.
L405: This trial enrolled a relatively healthy population. The minority of subjects were taking lipid-lowering medications prior to wash-out, ranging from 29% to 39% across groups. Similarly, the minority had BP &gt;140/90 or were on antihypertensive therapy at baseline, ranging from 28% to 43% across groups. Only one subject in the trial (randomized to ezetimibe monotherapy) had a history of atherosclerotic heart disease.
L406: Table 22 summarizes the disposition of subjects in study A-001. The drop-out rate was high for a 12-week study, with the highest incidence of premature discontinuation in the lomitapide monotherapy arm (32.1%). With the exception of a single loss to follow-up, all drop-outs were the result of adverse effects, which will be described subsequently.

L420: Reviewer Comments:
L421: 1. The effects of lomitapide and ezetimibe appear additive, when used in combination, with regard to LDL-C, TC, Non-HDL-C, and apo B.
2. The effect of the lomitapide regimen itself can be estimated from the comparison of combination therapy vs. ezetimibe alone. The nominal P values indicate robust statistical significance with regard to lomitapide contributing to the lowering of LDL-C, TC, non-HDL-C, HDL-C, apo B, apo A1, and perhaps Lp(a) (the latter being less robust in the setting of multiple comparisons).
3. This lomitapide regimen did not demonstrate a convincing effect on lowering TG, at least using the studied doses for this duration of therapy.
4. The effect of lomitapide-induced reductions in HDL-C (and apo A1) on clinical outcomes is unknown.
L422: The observed effects on LDL-C at week 4 (i.e., after lomitapide 5 mg) and week 8 (i.e., after lomitapide 7.5 mg) are shown in Table 25.

L431: Adverse Events
L432: Table 27 presents an overall summary of the TEAEs in the ITT population. There was one SAE in this trial, which was fatal, in the lomitapide monotherapy arm (described below). Nine (32%) of the 28 subjects assigned to lomitapide monotherapy discontinued because of an AE compared with 4 (14%) in each of the other two arms (lomitapide+ezetimibe and ezetimibe monotherapy). TEAEs occurred in 24 (86%), 24 (86%), and 16 (55%) subjects of the lomitapide monotherapy, lomitapide+ezetimibe combination therapy, and ezetimibe monotherapy groups, respectively.

L435: AE = adverse event
L436: Source Data: End-of-Text Tables 14.3.1.1, 14.3.1.4.1–14.3.1.4.3, 14.3.1.6, 14.3.2.1, and 14.3.2.3.
L437: Source: A-001 CSR, Table 18.
L438: The only fatal SAE in the lomitapide development program (myocardial infarction) occurred in this trial. This event is described in Section 7.3.1 (p. 158). There were no other SAEs.
L439: In study A-001, abnormal transaminases were the most common reason for premature discontinuation of study drug among subjects who received lomitapide. Among the nine (32%) subjects who discontinued lomitapide monotherapy, the AEs responsible for discontinuation included increased ALT (n=5), increased AST (n=2), and one event each of “increased hepatic enzyme,” abdominal discomfort, diarrhea, and deep vein thrombosis. Among the four (14%) subjects who discontinued lomitapide+ezetimibe combination therapy, the AEs responsible for discontinuation included “liver function test abnormal” (n=2) and one event each of increased ALT, diarrhea, and muscle spasms. Last, among the four subjects (14%) who discontinued ezetimibe monotherapy, the AEs responsible for discontinuation included one event each of increased creatinine, constipation, nausea, myalgia, and dizziness.
L440: Given that this was an active-controlled trial without a placebo group, I have not tabulated common adverse events. In general, observed non-serious AEs were consistent with other trials in the lomitapide development program. The most commonly observed TEAEs by preferred term in the combination therapy group were diarrhea (36%), increased ALT (18%), vomiting (14%), increased AST, and abnormal liver function test and upper respiratory tract infection (11% each). The most commonly observed in the lomitapide monotherapy group were diarrhea (39%); increased ALT (29%); increased AST (25%); dyspepsia, flatulence, and nausea (14% each); and abdominal pain, constipation, and eructation (11% each). The most commonly observed in the ezetimibe monotherapy group were abdominal distension, abdominal pain, back pain, diarrhea, dizziness, dyspepsia, and nasopharyngitis, each reported by 2 subjects (i.e., 7% each).
L441: Liver-related Laboratory Values
L442: In A-001, adverse events of elevated transaminases were reported for 6 (21%) of 28 subjects in the lomitapide+ezetimibe combination arm, 10 (36%) of 28 subjects in the lomitapide monotherapy arm, and no subjects in the ezetimibe monotherapy arm. Included among these, 3 (11%) subjects in the combination arm and 6 (21%) in the lomitapide monotherapy arm were withdrawn from the study because of elevated transaminases.
L443: Ten (36%) of the 28 subjects assigned to lomitapide monotherapy and seven (25%) of the 28 subjects assigned to lomitapide+ezetimibe combination therapy had a peak ALT \geq 3\times ULN after randomization; no subject assigned to ezetimibe monotherapy had an ALT elevation to this degree (Table 28). All 17 of these subjects had bilirubin levels &lt; 1.2\times ULN and normal INR.

L447: Reviewer Comments:
L448: 1. The clinical study report states that 4 subjects in the combination therapy group and 5 subjects in the lomitapide monotherapy group had confirmed ALT values &gt; 3\timesULN, where confirmation was defined as an elevation found on 2 consecutive tests. What the report does not mention, however, is that 3 of the “unconfirmed” elevations in each of the lomitapide monotherapy group and combination therapy group – which I have included in the table above – occurred at the week 12 end-of-treatment visit; therefore, these subjects discontinued therapy per protocol at the time of the elevation &gt; 3\timesULN and their levels fell below 3\timesULN when they returned for unscheduled follow-up visits. Regarding the other two “unconfirmed” elevations, subject 04-1062 had an ALT of 3.8\timesULN on study day 42, which led to discontinuation of lomitapide 7.5 mg monotherapy two days later; the next ALT value, obtained 8 days after drug discontinuation, was 2.0\timesULN. The last “unconfirmed” elevation occurred in subject 01-1061 three days after discontinuing lomitapide+ezetimibe combination therapy at week 12 per protocol. At this subject's end-of-treatment visit (day 86), ALT was 1.3\times ULN. It was rechecked on day 99 and found to be 7.1\timesULN, which fell to 2\timesULN by day 113. This last subject's ALT elevation is the least likely to have been induced by lomitapide simply based on the relationship to last dose, but additional data to support an alternative cause were not provided.
2. These data demonstrate that escalating the dose of lomitapide even more slowly than performed in the HoFH-pivotal trial does not prevent the elevation of
L449: transaminases, which occurred in 30% of all subjects who received lomitapide in a 12-week period.
L450: Figure 7 shows the longitudinal profiles of ALT (relative to ULN) for only the 17 subjects who ever had ALT \geq 3\times ULN (treatment arms are not distinguished). Colored lines indicate days during which the subject was on-treatment according to the submitted exposure dataset (compliance not considered); black dashed lines continue the profiles across any off-treatment follow-up visits. Recall that all of these subjects would be expected to be taking regimens including lomitapide 5 mg between baseline and week 4, 7.5 mg between weeks 4 and 8, and 10 mg between weeks 8 and 12.
L451: Figure 7: A-001 – ALT Profiles for Subjects with Peak ALT ≥3x ULN. The graph shows ALT (xULN) on the y-axis (logarithmic scale from 1 to 8) versus Study Day on the x-axis (from -35 to 113). Vertical lines mark Baseline (day 0), Week 4 (day 28), Week 8 (day 56), and Week 12 (day 84). Multiple colored lines represent individual subject profiles during treatment, showing a general upward trend in ALT levels starting around day 0, peaking between days 28 and 56, and then declining. Black dashed lines represent post-treatment follow-up, showing a return towards baseline ALT levels for most subjects.
L452: Figure 7. A-001 – ALT Profiles for Subjects with Peak ALT \geq 3\times ULN
L453: Source: FDA clinical reviewer's analysis of submitted A-001 datasets (LB and EX.xpt). Black, dashed lines indicate values following treatment discontinuation. Lomitapide monotherapy and combination therapy groups are combined.
L454: Reviewer Comments:
L455: 1. This figure shows that this study cannot contribute information regarding how ALT responds with continued dosing, since study drug was universally discontinued among subjects who had ALT \geq 3\times ULN.2. ALT typically fell substantially within 2-4 weeks of discontinuing study drug.3. Elevations in ALT were observed early in the trial, when subjects were taking lomitapide 5 mg daily.
L456: Hepatic Fat
L457: Hepatic fat was not measured in study A-001.
L458: Vitamin E
L459: Fat-soluble nutrients were not measured in study A-001.
L460: Laboratory Results
L461: Measures of central tendency of hematology, serum chemistry, urinalysis, and coagulation test results did not suggest clinically significant differences between treatment groups with the exception of transaminases, which are discussed above. The relevant data in the A-001 CSR are Tables 14.3.3.1-3.
L462: Other than transaminases, post-baseline laboratory values marked as “clinically significant” by investigators included increased creatinine (1 subject in combination therapy arm and 1 subject in ezetimibe monotherapy arm), increased glucose in 2 subjects in the lomitapide monotherapy arm, and increased WBC in 1 subject in the ezetimibe monotherapy arm.
L463: Vital Signs
L464: The clinical study report states “There were no differences between the 3 groups and no clinically meaningful changes between baseline and the last visit in vital sign measurements,” but no tables of descriptive statistics for vital signs across study visit were submitted for this trial.
L465: Weight Change
L466: Among the 19 subjects in the lomitapide monotherapy arm who completed the week 12 visit, the mean (SD) change in weight from baseline was -0.7 (2.0) kg, or -1.0% from baseline. Among the 24 subjects in the ezetimibe monotherapy arm, the mean change in weight was -0.2 (1.8) kg, or -0.1% from baseline. Among the 24 subjects in the lomitapide+ezetimibe combination arm, the mean change in weight was -1.4 (2.6) kg, or -1.4% from baseline. None of the between-group comparisons were statistically significant (all P \geq 0.077).
L467: Pulmonary Function Tests
L468: Pulmonary function was not assessed in study A-001.
L469: Study AEGR-733-003b
L470: In 2007, Aegerion sponsored study AEGR-733-003b (“A-003b”) to compare 8 weeks of fixed-dose lomitapide (5 or 10 mg daily) in combination with atorvastatin 20 mg daily with both placebo and monotherapy of each agent in adult subjects with hyperlipidemia as described above for study A-001. Subjects were counseled to implement a low-fat diet (&lt;30% calories from fat).
L471: Reviewer Comments:
L472: 1. Comparing effect sizes of lomitapide to atorvastatin, or even comparing the incremental effect of lomitapide on background atorvastatin, is not generalizable to the HoFH population because of the attenuated effect of statins in patients with dysfunctional LDL receptors.2. Initially, this study was initiated as AEGR-733-003 on 17 May 2007 and new enrollment was stopped prematurely on 28 June 2007 because of GI AEs that
L473: were more severe than expected based on previous studies. The sponsor identified contamination with Bacillus cereus in the AEG-733 capsules. After correcting this issue of contamination, the protocol was re-initiated as A-003b, described below. Other than confirming that there were no SAEs or deaths reported, I did not review the details of the prematurely terminated trial.
L474: A-003b Demographics &amp; Disposition
L475: Of the 157 subjects enrolled, 70 (45%) were men; 127 (81%) were white and 23 (15%) were black. The mean (SD) age was 54 (10) years with a range of 22 to 70 years. The mean (SD) BMI was 28.8 (4.6) kg/m2. Mean (SD) LDL-C at baseline was 173 (28) mg/dL with a range of 104 to 280 mg/dL.
L476: This trial enrolled a relatively healthy population. The sum of the number of NCEP risk factors (see inclusion criteria above), subtracting one for HDL-C \geq 60 mg/dL, was 0 for 18%, 1 for 29%, 2 for 35%, 3 for 12%, 4 for 1%, and 5 for 1%. Only 4 (2.5%) of the 157 subjects reported a prior history of atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease.
L477: The minority of subjects were taking lipid-lowering medications prior to wash-out. My analysis of the submitted concomitant medications dataset (CM.xpt), for example, showed that only 25% of subjects had been on statins (6 or 7 subjects in each group had a history of statin use). Similarly, the minority had BP &gt; 140/90 or were on antihypertensive therapy at baseline, ranging from 27% to 46% across groups.
L478: Table 29 summarizes the disposition of subjects in study A-003b. The drop-out rate was very high for an 8-week study, with the highest incidence of premature discontinuation in the groups that included lomitapide 10 mg daily (62% and 69% drop-out for monotherapy and combination therapy, respectively). Premature discontinuation was largely the result of adverse events (predominantly diarrhea).

L484: Reviewer Comments:
L485: 1. According to the submitted line listings, the “other” discontinuation was “Subject (05123) removed from the study due to lab values.” A review of the laboratory dataset shows that this subject had ALT values of 32, 213, and 233 IU/L at weeks 2, 4, and 6, respectively. The subject discontinued study drug the day after the week 6 visit and was withdrawn from the study approximately 2 weeks later when the ALT was 272 IU/L. An off-study followup visit 43 days later documented resolution of ALT to 32 IU/L.
2. Reasons for withdrawal of consent were not collected.
L486: A-003b Efficacy Results
L487: Table 30 summarizes the mean baseline values for lipid parameters in trial A-003b, and Table 31 summarizes the least squares means of % changes in lipid parameters after 8 weeks of treatment. These values only include subjects with available data (i.e., no imputation), and the proportion of each group with missing data because of premature discontinuation is substantial (e.g., 65% missing in L10+A20 group).

I have, however, included the atorvastatin monotherapy vs. lomitapide+atorvastatin combinations for completeness.

L496: The sponsor did not perform pairwise t-test comparisons between groups for TG since the overall one-way ANOVA P=0.48, suggesting that the null hypothesis of no difference between groups in mean % change in TG could not be rejected.
L497: * P&lt;0.05; ** P&lt;0.01; † P&lt;0.001; ‡ P &lt;0.0001 with the P group being the referent for L5 and L10, and the A20 group being the referent for L5+A20 and L10+A20.
L498: § For TC, TG, ApoB, HDL-C, and ApoAI, n=26 for P, n=19 for L5, and n=25 for A20.
L499: Reviewer Comments:
L500: 1. At least among subjects who were able to stay on drug for the duration of the 8-week trial, lomitapide caused statistically significant reductions in LDL-C, TC, non-HDL-C, apoB, HDL-C, and apoAI. Furthermore, in this fixed-dose study, there appears to be a dose-response from 5 mg to 10 mg among completers.2. These results are akin to a per-protocol analysis, as the clinical study report states that “blood draws for lipid and other efficacy measures (not safety) were only drawn if the study subject had received study medication in the past 3 days.” This has the potential to introduce bias in favor of lomitapide.3. This study does not provide evidence for an effect of lomitapide on TG, at least at the doses and duration studied.4. Note that the placebo-subtracted change in HDL-C from baseline to week 8 is -12% and -19.1% for lomitapide 5 mg and 10 mg, respectively, which could plausibly increase CV risk.5. At least at the low doses of lomitapide studied, this phase 2 study does not provide convincing evidence of benefit for lomitapide when added to atorvastatin 20 mg daily among non-HoFH hyperlipidemic subjects. These comparisons are not relevant for HoFH patients, however, since patients with LDLR dysfunction would not be expected to achieve a mean 40% reduction in LDL-C with moderate-dose atorvastatin.
L501: The mean % change in LDL-C over time for each group is shown in Figure 8. Recall that no imputation was performed and there was substantial dropout in lomitapide-treated groups.

L506: A-003b Safety Results
L507: The sponsor did not summarize exposure data for A-003b in the clinical study report. I used the submitted drug exposure dataset to describe the exposure to active lomitapide or placebo by group (Table 32).

L510: Source: FDA clinical reviewer's analysis of submitted A-003b exposure dataset (EX.xpt)* One subject in the L5 group did not have an end date recorded.
L511: Reviewer Comment: Note the high incidence of early discontinuation in lomitapide-treated groups, especially at the 10 mg dose (e.g., 7 [27%] of the 26 subjects in the L10 group stopped study drug on or before study day 5). Thus, the incidence of any AEs that are associated with longer exposure to study drug may be underestimated in these groups.
L512: Adverse Events
L513: Table 33 presents an overall summary of the treatment-emergent adverse events in the ITT population. There were two SAEs in this trial (one myocardial infarction in each of the lomitapide monotherapy arms), described in Section 7.3.2 (p. 159), and there were no deaths.
L514: Among the 52 subjects assigned to a treatment arm that included lomitapide 5 mg, 14 (27%) discontinued because of an AE. Among the 52 subjects assigned to an arm that included lomitapide 10 mg, 31 (60%) discontinued because of an AE. In contrast, no subject assigned to either placebo or atorvastatin monotherapy discontinued because of an AE.

L517: Source: Derived from A-003b CSR, Table 12-1.
L518: TEAE = treatment-emergent adverse event; “Severe” intensity of AE was assigned by the investigator; SAE = serious adverse events
L519: Reviewer Comment: Even at low doses (5-10 mg) for short-term therapy (8 weeks), lomitapide is not well tolerated in the general population, with discontinuation rates of ~20-65% observed in this phase 2 trial. Furthermore, even though severity of AEs is subjective, the incidence of severe AEs is quite high.
L520: Table 123 (Appendix) summarizes the adverse events that led to study discontinuation in A-003b by system organ class and preferred term. The most common causes for premature discontinuation were gastrointestinal symptoms (predominantly diarrhea). Subject counts of treatment-emergent AEs that were more reported by ≥5% of subjects (i.e., ≥2 subjects) and were more common in a lomitapide (with or without atorvastatin) group than a control (placebo or atorvastatin) group are listed in Table 124 (Appendix).
L521: Reviewer Comments:
L522: 1. Consistent with other studies, the most common lomitapide-associated AEs are gastrointestinal symptoms and abnormalities of hepatic transaminases.
2. There is no further information available for 3 cases of “chest pain” in the lomitapide 5 mg monotherapy group. There were no cases of “chest pain” in any of the three other lomitapide groups. The investigators classified these events as mild/possibly related, mild/unrelated, and moderate/unlikely related.
L523: Liver-related Laboratory Values
L524: In contrast to the preceding clinical trials that discontinued study drug for transaminases &gt;3x ULN, this trial implemented the following liver-related safety criteria:
L525: • ALT and/or AST &gt;10x ULN and/or bilirubin &gt;2x ULN: discontinue and follow until resolution or stabilization;
• ALT and/or AST &gt;5x ULN, alkaline phosphatase &gt;3x ULN, or bilirubin 1.5x ULN (unless evidence of underlying Gilbert’s syndrome): confirm result within 7-14 days and discontinue study medication if repeat values exceed these thresholds
L526: until ALT or AST &lt;2x ULN, alkaline phosphatase &lt;1.5x ULN, or bilirubin &lt;1.0x ULN, at which point the drug could be restarted;
L527: • ALT and/or AST &gt;2x but &lt;5x ULN and otherwise asymptomatic, and/or bilirubin &gt;1.0x but &lt;1.5x ULN (with normal pre-treatment levels): carefully follow for further evaluation but continue on study drug.
L528: In A-003b, adverse events of elevated transaminases (i.e., combined preferred terms of ALT increased, AST increased, hepatic enzymes increased, liver function test abnormal, and transaminases increased) were reported for 1 subject (4%) in each of the placebo and atorvastatin monotherapy groups; 2 (8%) and 5 (19%) subjects in the lomitapide 5 mg and 10 mg monotherapy groups, respectively; and 2 (8%) and 1 (4%) subject in the lomitapide 5 mg and 10 mg in combination with atorvastatin groups, respectively. Included among these, 4 (15%) of 26 subjects in the lomitapide 10 mg monotherapy group and 1 (4%) subject in each of the lomitapide + atorvastatin groups were withdrawn for elevated transaminases.
L529: There was one case of ALT \geq 3x ULN in the placebo group in a subject who had normal transaminases through the week 6 study visit (range, 9-14 IU/L) and then had an ALT 75 IU/L (3.0x ULN) at the final study visit at week 8. There were no cases of ALT \geq 3x ULN in the atorvastatin monotherapy group. In contrast, 24% of subjects treated with lomitapide monotherapy 10 mg daily had a peak ALT \geq 3x ULN during the 8 week trial, even given the high proportion of early discontinuations in this group (Table 34). Longitudinal ALT profiles for these subjects are shown in Figure 9.
L530: Concomitant total bilirubin levels, alkaline phosphatase, and INR levels were normal for all subjects with ALT \geq 3x ULN.

L541: Vitamin A &amp; E
L542: Vitamins A and E were measured at screening, baseline, and at each study visit during trial A-003b. I did not identify any post-baseline values that were below the LLN for any subject.

L550: Reviewer Comment: Consistent with other trials in the development program, lomitapide induced a dose-related decrease in total vitamin E, but not vitamin A, in this 8-week study. Although it's possible that the subjects who discontinued prematurely may have been more likely to have shown decreases in vitamin A, the fact that there does not even appear to be a modest decrease in mean vitamin A levels among completers is reassuring, although this study used low doses of lomitapide for a relatively short duration.

L563: Reviewer Comment: The completer population does not demonstrate any concerning lomitapide-associated trends with regard to changes in heart rate or blood pressure during 8 weeks of treatment.
L564: Weight Change
L565: Among completers, the % changes in body weight from baseline to week 8 were -0.01% for placebo (n=26), -2.5% for lomitapide 5 mg monotherapy (n=19), and -3.5% for lomitapide 10 mg monotherapy (n=10) (P \leq 0.0005 for both comparisons). Regarding the atorvastatin-containing groups, the % changes in body weight were -0.4% for atorvastatin 20 mg monotherapy (n=25), -1.8% for lomitapide 5 mg + atorvastatin 20 mg (n=18), and -2.8% for lomitapide 10 mg + atorvastatin 20 mg (n=10) (P=0.04 and P=0.008 for L5+A20 and L10+A20 vs. atorvastatin monotherapy, respectively).
L566: Pulmonary Function Tests
L567: Pulmonary function was not assessed in study A-003b.
L568: Study AEGR-733-004
L569: Pursuing these lower doses of lomitapide, Aegerion conducted study AEGR-733-004 (“A-004”) in 2007-2008 to better characterize hepatic fat accumulation and to explore whether the use of concomitant medications (atorvastatin, fenofibrate, or ezetimibe) may attenuate this accumulation. This 12-week study was the longest-duration placebo-controlled trial in the lomitapide development program.
L570: Study A-004 enrolled 260 adults with LDL-C \geq 100 and &lt; 190 mg/dL (after a 4-week washout of lipid-lowering agents) who had hepatic fat &lt; 6.2\% by NMRS at screening. Similar to study C-009 described above, which also assessed lomitapide-induced hepatic fat, there were multiple exclusion criteria; these included a history of diabetes mellitus, regular alcohol use (&gt; 1 drink per day), liver disease or transaminases &gt; \text{ULN} at screening, and BMI &gt; 35 kg/m2. Following the screening and washout period, during which a low-fat (&lt; 30\% total calories from fat) was implemented, subjects were randomly assigned to one of 8 treatment arms for 12 weeks: placebo; fixed-dose lomitapide monotherapy at either 2.5, 5, 7.5, or 10 mg daily; or lomitapide 5 mg daily in combination with daily doses of either atorvastatin 20 mg, micronized fenofibrate 145 mg, or ezetimibe 10 mg. There was no scheduled off-treatment follow-up visit. Hepatic fat was evaluated with MRS/MRI at baseline and weeks 4, 8, and 12.
L571: A-004 Demographics &amp; Disposition
L572: Of the 260 subjects enrolled, 124 (48%) were men; 182 (70%) were white, 55 (21%) were black, 19 (7%) were Hispanic, and 3 (1%) were Asian. The mean (SD) age was 51 (11) years with a range of 20 to 70 years. The mean (SD) BMI was 27.3 (3.7) kg/m2. Mean (SD) waist circumference was 92 (11) cm. Mean (SD) LDL-C at baseline was 143 (28) mg/dL with a range of 85 to 317 mg/dL.
L573: This trial enrolled a relatively healthy population. Based on NCEP risk stratification, the majority of subjects in each group had 0 or 1 CHD risk factor (67 to 91%). Five subjects had a history of prior CHD or atherosclerotic disease.
L574: The minority of subjects were taking lipid-lowering medications prior to wash-out. The clinical study report does not report previous statin use, but my analysis of the submitted concomitant medications dataset (CM.xpt) showed that only 28 (11%) of the 260 subjects had been on statins (1 to 6 subjects in each group had a history of statin use). Similarly, the minority had a history of hypertension (12% to 24% across groups).
L575: Table 39 summarizes the disposition of subjects in study A-004. Similar to other phase 2 studies for lomitapide, premature discontinuation was relatively common among lomitapide-treated subjects, primarily because of adverse events (predominantly diarrhea).

L583: Reviewer Comments: This was a fixed-dose trial, which may have affected tolerability for the higher doses of lomitapide (i.e., 10 mg daily) according to the sponsor.
L584: A-004 Efficacy Results
L585: Hepatic Fat
L586: The primary endpoint for trial A-004 was the absolute change in % hepatic fat from baseline to week 12 for lomitapide 5 mg daily vs. placebo. The placebo-subtracted change from baseline was a 4.7% (95% CI, 2.3% to 7.1%) absolute percentage point increase in % hepatic fat (p &lt; 0.001); the placebo group showed no change during the 12 weeks (mean +0.03%). Table 40 summarizes the mean and median changes from baseline to week 12 across lomitapide monotherapy groups compared with placebo.

L618: Reviewer Comment: Although the sponsor suggests that the correlation between change in LDL-C and hepatic fat in the monotherapy treatment groups was “weak,” these data do show that the subjects who have the greatest increases in hepatic fat also have the greatest reductions in LDL-C, in general. The long-term consequence of this hepatic fat accumulation is the unanswerable question with the currently available data.
L619: One concern regarding the accumulation of hepatic fat is the association between insulin resistance and hepatic fat. It is generally believed that, in the general population, the accumulation of hepatic fat is a consequence of insulin resistance. Some have suggested, however, that hepatic fat itself might induce insulin resistance. Regarding this possibility, I note that the sponsor has examined cross-sectional associations between % change in C-peptide and change in % hepatic fat. Combining all lomitapide-monotherapy groups and all post-baseline visits, there was no evidence for an association between these measures (R^2 = 0.1\%) (Figure 13).
L620: A scatter plot showing the relationship between the change in % hepatic fat by MRS on the x-axis and the % change in C-peptide on the y-axis. The x-axis ranges from -5 to 45, and the y-axis ranges from -100 to 300. A horizontal line is drawn at y=0. The data points are scattered around this line, with a few outliers. The coefficient of determination, R^{**2} = 0.001, is displayed in the upper right corner of the plot area.Scatter plot showing the relationship between Change in % Hepatic Fat by MRS (X-axis) and % Change in C Peptide (Y-axis). The plot shows a weak correlation with R**2 = 0.001.
L621: Data source: Section 14.2, Figure 15.3.6
L622: Figure 13. A-004 – Change in % Hepatic Fat vs. % Change in C-peptideSource: A-004 CSR, Figure 10.
L623: Reviewer Comment: Although a repeated-measures model would have been more appropriate given that within-subject observations are not independent over time, I agree that data from this trial do not seem to suggest an association between lomitapide-induced hepatic fat accumulation and changes in C-peptide, providing some reassurance that endogenous insulin levels do not increase substantially with the development of hepatic fat, at least with the use of low-dose lomitapide (\leq 10 mg daily) for \leq 12 weeks.
L624: In an additional exploratory analysis, I examined the association between maximum change in % hepatic fat during the study in the lomitapide monotherapy groups with
L625: Best Available Copy
L626: absolute change in HOMA-IR as a measure of insulin resistance. Subjects without baseline insulin values were excluded from the analysis (~25% in each group). Although I did not apply any statistical tests, it's apparent that those with the largest increases in hepatic fat did not typically exhibit increases in insulin resistance, at least as measured by the HOMA-IR score (Figure 14).

L641: Reviewer Comments:
L642: 1. These data support a dose-dependent effect of lomitapide on the reduction of LDL-C, total cholesterol, and apoB.
2. This trial failed to detect a statistically significant effect of lomitapide on TG compared with placebo. The mean and median % changes from baseline to week 12/LOCF in the placebo group were +11.0% and +3%, respectively, corresponding to mean and median absolute changes of +4.6 and 3 mg/dL. For the lomitapide monotherapy groups, the median absolute within-group changes from baseline to week 12/LOCF were -5, +1, -18, and -13 mg/dL for the lomitapide 2.5, 5, 7.5, and 10 mg daily groups, respectively. Thus, the effect of lomitapide on fasting TG is modest at these doses, at best, and highly variable.
L643: HDL-C values dropped rather abruptly after the initiation of lomitapide with a return toward baseline over time (Figure 15). A similar profile was not observed for LDL-C, which decreased during the first 2 weeks and remained reasonably stable through week 12, with the most variability noted in the lomitapide 10 mg group (Figure 16).

L660: Source: A-004 CSR, Table 12-1.
L661: TEAE = treatment-emergent adverse event.
L662: * SAE = serious adverse event. For this table, these events are limited to those occurring after first dose of lomitapide. Two additional subjects had SAEs after enrollment but prior to first dose of study drug.
L663: Among the 227 subjects who received any dose of lomitapide, 41 (18%) discontinued because of an AE. In contrast, one (3%) of the 33 subjects assigned to placebo discontinued because of an AE. The majority of discontinuations (41 of 42) occurred in subjects who received lomitapide (monotherapy or in combination). Similar to other studies, adverse events in the Gastrointestinal Disorders SOC contributed the most to premature discontinuation. Diarrhea contributed to 26 of 42 subjects discontinuing prematurely. Other GI disorders leading to discontinuation included nausea (n=7), upper abdominal pain (n=6), abdominal pain (n=5), abdominal distension (n=2), and stomach discomfort (n=2). Three subjects discontinued as a result of arthralgia and three subjects as a result of fatigue. One subject in the lomitapide 10 mg monotherapy group and one in the lomitapide 5 mg + fenofibrate group discontinued because of elevated transaminases. Discontinuations as a result of AEs occurred most frequently in the lomitapide 10 mg group (31%).
L664: Because this is the longest placebo-controlled trial in the lomitapide development program, I have presented the subject counts of TEAEs that were reported by ≥5% of subjects (i.e., ≥2 subjects) in a lomitapide monotherapy group and were more frequent
L665: than placebo (Table 48). I have not included arms of combination therapy in this table for brevity and because of the lack of an active-control group.

L669: Events with a non-missing start date prior to first dose are excluded.
L670: A preferred term is listed only if it was reported by ≥5% of subjects (i.e., ≥2 subjects) and by more subjects in a lomitapide group than the placebo group.
L671: Reviewer Comment: The AE profile in this trial is generally consistent with other phase 2 trials in the development program.
L672: Liver-related Laboratory Values
L673: In A-004, 11 (5%) of 227 subjects treated with any dose of lomitapide (monotherapy and combination therapy groups combined) had at least one AE reported of increased ALT and/or increased AST: 3 (9%) of the lomitapide 2.5 mg group, 2 (6%) of the lomitapide 5 mg group, 1 (3%) of the lomitapide 7.5 mg group, 1 (3%) of the lomitapide 10 mg group, and 4 (12%) of the lomitapide 5 mg + fenofibrate group. There were no AEs of increased transaminases reported for the placebo group or the lomitapide 5 mg in combination with either atorvastatin or ezetimibe groups.
L674: Using the laboratory dataset, I identified peak ALT values for each subject and categorized them relative to the laboratory's ULN (Table 49). No subjects in the placebo group had an ALT \geq 2\times ULN. Use of lomitapide was associated with elevated transaminases, and although there wasn't a definite dose-response across the range of 2.5 to 10 mg doses, the lomitapide 10 mg daily group had the highest proportion of markedly elevated transaminases (3 [9%] of 35 subjects with peak ALT values of 10.8x, 11.6x, and 11.7x ULN).
L675: Concomitant total bilirubin, alkaline phosphatase, and INR levels were &lt;1.5\times ULN for all except 2 subjects with ALT \geq 3\times ULN:
L676: Subject 10013 (lomitapide 10 mg) had an ALT 290 IU/L (11.6x ULN) at the final study visit at week 12 (at the preceding week 10 visit, ALT was 22 IU/L). At week 12, alkaline phosphatase was 132 U/L (1.8x ULN) but total bilirubin was 0.3 mg/dL and INR 0.9.
Subject 04016 (lomitapide 5 mg + fenofibrate) had an ALT 112 IU/L (4.5x ULN) at week 4 with an alkaline phosphatase 125 U/L (1.7x ULN), but total bilirubin 0.6 mg/dL and INR 0.9.

L680: Vitamins A &amp; E
L681: In trial A-004, Vitamins A and E were measured at every study visit from baseline through week 12 (end-of-study) or early termination.
L682: There were no statistically significant changes in Vitamin A from baseline to week 12 (with or without LOCF), compared with placebo. Although the large variability and small sample size limited power to detect differences, the point estimates for the mean absolute changes from baseline were modest and did not show a convincing dose-response. Baseline mean Vitamin A levels among the placebo and lomitapide monotherapy groups ranged from 59.6 to 62.4 \mu\text{g/dL}; at week 12/LOCF, the absolute
L683: mean (SD) change from baseline was +1.8 (12.0) \mu\text{g/dL} in the placebo group and +0.2 (11.3), +2.6 (8.03), -2.0 (12.1), and +1.7 (9.7) \mu\text{g/dL} in the lomitapide 2.5, 5, 7.5, and 10 mg monotherapy groups, respectively.
L684: There were statistically significant reductions in total Vitamin E from baseline to week 12 (with or without LOCF) in the lomitapide 5, 7.5, and 10 mg monotherapy groups compared with placebo. (The groups with lomitapide 5 mg in combination with atorvastatin, fenofibrate, and ezetimibe also demonstrated statistically significant reductions compared with placebo, but these groups will not be discussed further.) Baseline mean Vitamin E levels among the placebo and lomitapide monotherapy groups ranged from 1.4 to 1.6 mg/dL; at week 12/LOCF, the absolute mean (SD) change from baseline was +0.1 (0.32) mg/dL in the placebo group and -0.1 (0.36), -0.2 (0.27), -0.2 (0.33), and -0.3 (0.39) mg/dL in the lomitapide 2.5, 5, 7.5, and 10 mg monotherapy groups, respectively. No subject decreased below the LLN (0.50 mg/dL) during the study.
L685: The sponsor performed a post hoc analysis of the change in the ratio between Vitamin E and lipids (Vitamin E:TC+TG). Baseline mean ratios among the placebo and lomitapide monotherapy groups ranged from 4.22 to 4.68; at week 12/LOCF, the absolute mean (SD) change from baseline was +0.31 (0.93) in the placebo group and +0.04 (0.62), -0.24 (0.85), +0.04 (0.68), and -0.18 (1.16) in the lomitapide 2.5, 5, 7.5, and 10 mg monotherapy groups, respectively.
L686: Reviewer Comment: The longitudinal profile of vitamin E mirrors those of the lipid profiles – a reduction during the first 4 weeks followed by relatively stability or slight return toward baseline. This is consistent with the association between vitamin E and lipids, especially those containing apoB, which are necessary for its transport. It is reassuring both that vitamin E did not fall below the LLN despite its decrease and that the vitamin E:lipid ratio remained stable.
L687: Laboratory Results
L688: Table 50 summarizes the mean (SD) values for safety laboratories at week 12/LOCF in the placebo and lomitapide monotherapy groups. Baseline mean values were similar between groups (not shown). I have not presented the lomitapide combination groups because of a lack of active controls; I did review results from these groups, however, and they did not raise safety concerns. Furthermore, the differences between the mean values with and without LOCF were modest and did not change the interpretation of these results.

L698: Reviewer Comment: No concerning signals based on central tendency of vital signs were detected in this trial. Furthermore, in this trial, the ECG ventricular rate did not change from baseline to end-of-study in the lomitapide 10 mg group (66.4 bpm and 66.3 bpm, respectively, which was actually a -1.0 bpm placebo-subtracted change).

L709: Reviewer Comment: These data do not suggest a lomitapide-associated change in pulmonary function, at least as measured by spirometry and DLCO after 12 weeks of exposure at dosages ≤10 mg daily.
L710: Study AEGR-733-006
L711: The last phase 2 trial in Aegerion's development program for lomitapide aimed to investigate whether adding a low dose of lomitapide to atorvastatin 20 mg daily would reduce LDL-C more than atorvastatin alone. Trial A-003b had studied fixed-dose lomitapide 5 mg or 10 mg in combination with atorvastatin 20 mg daily for 8 weeks. In study AEGR-733-006 ("A-006"), they used a dose-escalation strategy of 2.5 mg daily x 4 weeks followed by 5 mg daily x 4 weeks.
L712: Given that this study was short-duration, active-controlled, and used doses that are largely irrelevant to the HoFH indication under review, only a brief summary follows.
L713: A-006 Summary of Results
L714: This 8-week trial enrolled 44 subjects (23 atorvastatin monotherapy; 21 atorvastatin + lomitapide) and 41 completed the study. The three premature discontinuations were for AEs: two in the combination group and one in the atorvastatin monotherapy group (see below).
L715: Overall, the mean (SD) age was 59 (7) years, 46% were men, and 42 (96%) of 44 were white. The median sum of NCEP cardiac risk factors was 2. The mean (SD) LDL-C at baseline was 178 (29) mg/dL, with a range of 109 to 227 mg/dL.
L716: Table 54 summarizes the changes in lipid parameters for each group from baseline to weeks 4 and 8, along with the P values for the between-group comparisons.

L720: Primary efficacy comparison is in bolded italics.

L722: Reviewer Comments:
L723: 1. These comparisons cannot be extrapolated to the HoFH population, as the incremental effect of lomitapide to a statin in the general population may be distinct from the incremental effect in the HoFH population where statins are less effective as a result of LDLR mutations.
L724: 2. Add-on lomitapide did not lower TG more than atorvastatin alone, at the doses and duration studied.3. Similar to other studies, reductions in HDL-C and apoAI were observed with the addition of low-dose lomitapide.
L725: With regard to safety, there were no treatment-emergent SAEs or deaths in this study. There was one SAE of diverticulitis in the atorvastatin group, but this was for an operation scheduled for abdominal symptoms that preceded study drug (and did not worsen on study drug). This SAE led to early discontinuation. The AEs leading to early discontinuation for 2 subjects in the combination therapy group were elevated ALT for Subject 03-009 (64 IU/L on day 0, 67 IU/L at week 1, and 82 IU/L at early termination visit), and gastrointestinal symptoms for Subject 05-009 (diarrhea, worsening heartburn, gas, stomach ache began on day 2, leading to discontinuation on day 8).
L726: Adverse events that occurred in at least 2 subjects and with greater frequency in the combination therapy group compared with atorvastatin alone were diarrhea (10 vs. 3 subjects), dyspepsia (3 vs. 0), flatulence (4 vs. 2), nausea (3 vs. 2); urinary tract infection (3 vs. 0); ALT increased (4 vs. 0); headache (3 vs. 2); and dermal cyst (2 vs. 0).
L727: Given the low doses of lomitapide used in this study, its short duration, and the fact that a clinically significant lomitapide-atorvastatin interaction is not expected at the doses studied (see Section 7.5.5, p. 242), I did not review all laboratories, vital signs, and ECGs in detail. Shift tables and measures of central tendency did reveal increases in ALT and AST, however, similar to other studies. There were 3 subjects in the combination therapy group with peak ALT levels \geq 3\times ULN (4.5x, 4.7x, and 4.8x); two occurred at week 8 (final visit) and one at week 6. There were no concomitant increases in total bilirubin, alkaline phosphatase, or INR. No subjects in the atorvastatin monotherapy group had ALT levels \geq 3\times ULN.
L728: Phase 2 and 3 HoFH Trial Designs
L729: This section describes the study designs of the phase 3 pivotal trial and the phase 2 pilot trial of lomitapide in HoFH. The information herein was primarily derived from the study protocols themselves and not from the final clinical study reports. Efficacy and safety results from the HoFH trials are presented in Sections 6 and 7, respectively.
L730: Study UP1002/AEGR-733-005 (“HoFH-pivotal”)
L731: Title: “A phase III study of microsomal triglyceride transfer protein (MTP) inhibitor AEGR-733 in patients with homozygous familial hypercholesterolemia on current lipid-lowering therapy”
L732: Study Centers and Study Period
L733: Eleven study centers in United States (2), South Africa (3), Canada (2), and Italy (4).
L734: First informed consent: 18 December 2007
L735: Data cut-off for initial NDA submission (all had completed week 56): 12 April 2011
L736: Data cut-off for SAEs and other AEs of interest (on submission): 08 September 2011
L737: Data cut-off for 120-day safety update (all had completed week 78): 31 December 2011
L738: Trial Objectives &amp; Design
L739: Primary Objective
L740: • Evaluate the efficacy of lomitapide as defined by % change in LDL-C at the maximum tolerated dose (MTD) compared with baseline after 26 weeks of treatment in combination with other lipid-lowering therapy in patients with HoFH
L741: Secondary Objectives
L742: • To evaluate other lipid parameters, long-term safety, % change in hepatic fat, and PK of lomitapide in combination with other lipid-lowering agents in patients with HoFH as assessed by○ % change in TC, non-HDL-C, HDL-C, TG, VLDL, Lp(a), and apolipoproteins B (apoB) and A1 (apoA1)○ Changes in liver-associated enzymes (AST, ALT, total bilirubin, alkaline phosphatase)○ Number of subjects developing biopsy-proven evidence of steatohepatitis or other liver pathologies○ Changes in safety laboratories, reported AEs, physical exam, QT or QTc, serum levels of fat-soluble vitamins, serum levels of fatty acids, weight, hsCRP, hepatic fat, pulmonary function tests○ PK parameters
L743: Study Design
L744: • Phase 3 clinical trial to evaluate both the efficacy and long-term safety of lomitapide at the MTD• Single-arm multicenter trial involving 29 subjects with HoFH who received lomitapide following a protocol-specified escalation of dose to the MTD• Duration: ~6-week run-in period to stabilize current lipid-lowering therapy (including apheresis if applicable) + 78 weeks treatment, comprising a 26-week efficacy phase and a 52-week safety phase○ Note: At original NDA submission, complete data were presented through the week 56 assessment.• Dose-escalation: Daily doses of 5 mg x 2 weeks followed by 10, 20, 40, and 60 mg at 4-week intervals as tolerated; further titration to 80 mg was an option if certain safety and efficacy criteria were met• During the safety phase, the dose of lomitapide was not to be increased above the MTD established during the efficacy phase
L745: • Lipid-lowering therapy and LDL apheresis schedule were to remain constant during the efficacy phase (through week 26) but could be changed during the safety phase (week 26 onward).• Criteria with regard to measuring LDL-C in relation to LDL apheresis schedule were established.• Off-drug follow-up visit at week 84 (6 weeks post-treatment) for subjects who did not enroll in the optional extension study (AEGR-733-012; hereafter, “HoFH-extension”).
L746: Reviewer Comment: During the development program for lomitapide, the Agency agreed to the plausibility of a single-arm trial serving as the only pivotal phase 3 trial to support an indication in the HoFH population. The six-week run-in period, which was intended to stabilize concomitant lipid-lowering therapy, should reduce the magnitude of regression to the mean when assessing efficacy. Furthermore, placebo groups in other parallel-group randomized trials of lipid-lowering agents have generally exhibited very little change in LDL-C from baseline over time.
L747: Trial Population
L748: The protocol anticipated the enrollment of 25 adult subjects with HoFH.
L749: Inclusion Criteria (selected)
L750: • Males and females \geq 18 years of age• Diagnosis of functional homozygous FH by at least one of the following clinical criteria:○ documented functional mutation(s) in both LDL receptor alleles or alleles known to affect LDL receptor functionality, OR○ skin fibroblast LDL receptor activity &lt;20\% normal, OR○ untreated TC &gt;500 mg/dL and TG &lt;300 mg/dL AND both parents with documented untreated TC &gt;250 mg/dL• Body weight \geq 40 kg and &lt;136 kg (due to MRI weight limit)• Negative screening pregnancy test if female of child-bearing potential
L751: Exclusion Criteria (selected)
L752: • Uncontrolled hypertension: SBP &gt;180 mmHg, DBP &gt;95 mmHg on medication• History of chronic renal insufficiency (serum creatinine &gt;2.5 mg/dL)• History of biopsy-proven cirrhosis or abnormal LFTs at screening (AST or ALT &gt;2\times ULN and/or total bilirubin &gt;1.5 mg/dL unless patient has unconjugated hyperbilirubinemia due to Gilbert's syndrome)• Chronic hepatitis B or hepatitis C (positive for HBsAg or HepC Ab)• Male subjects reporting &gt;2 drinks/day or females reporting &gt;1 drink/day (1 drink = 12 oz beer, 1 oz hard liquor, 5 oz wine)• Any major surgical procedure &lt;3 months prior to screening visit• Cardiac insufficiency defined by NYHA Class III or IV• Previous organ transplantation• History of non-skin malignancy within previous 3 years
L753: • Known significant gastrointestinal bowel disease or malabsorption (e.g., inflammatory bowel disease or chronic pancreatitis requiring use of daily pancreatic enzymes)• Certain medications known to be potentially hepatotoxic, especially those that can induce microvesicular or macrovesicular steatosis. These include, but are not limited to, Accutane, amiodarone, heavy acetaminophen use (4g/day &gt;3x/week), methotrexate, tetracyclines, and tamoxifen• Documented diagnosis of any of the following pulmonary conditions: asthma, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis• Documented diagnosis of any of the following liver diseases: nonalcoholic steatohepatitis, alcoholic liver disease, autoimmune hepatitis, primary biliary cirrhosis, primary sclerosing cholangitis, Wilson's disease, hemochromatosis, \alpha_1-anti-trypsin deficiency.• Current use of corticosteroids or betaine
L754: Study Conduct &amp; Schedule
L755: Study Schedule Highlights
L756: At visit 1 (screening), potential subjects signed informed consent and underwent a history and physical, ECG, and blood/urine samples for laboratory testing. In addition, a dietitian met with subjects at visit 1 to instruct them to follow a diet supplying &lt;20% energy from fat; the dietitian was to review current eating habits, point out needed changes, address potential adherence problems, and review detailed diet instructions. Subjects were provided information to call or schedule a meeting with the dietitian on an as-needed basis during the study.
L757: Eligible subjects were contacted and instructed not to change concomitant lipid-lowering therapy for ≥6 weeks before baseline (and through week 26). After a minimum of 4 weeks from the start of this run-in period, subjects returned for visit 2 (week -2), at which time they were instructed to start taking dietary supplements of vitamin E and fatty acids.
L758: After completing at least 6 weeks of a run-in period, subjects returned for visit 3 (week 0), where they underwent the baseline physical examination, vital signs, ECG, laboratories, Gastrointestinal Symptom Rating Scale (GSRS) questionnaire, liver imaging (MRI/NMRS), and PFTs with DLCO. Two-day diet records were to be returned. Lomitapide 5 mg daily was initiated at this visit.
L759: Visits 4 through 10 occurred at weeks 2, 6, 10, 14, 18, 22, and 26 (± 3 days). The lomitapide dose was escalated at each visit, as tolerated (see below), through 5, 10, 20, 40, and 60 mg daily. Although LDL-C values were available to investigators, the protocol did not specify LDL-C criteria that would preclude dose escalation. The increase to 60 mg (intended maximum dose) was scheduled to occur at week 14 (visit 7) if the escalation occurred without complication, and primary efficacy was assessed at week 26 (visit 10). Each visit included a physical examination, vital signs, ECG, laboratory evaluation, GSRS, recording of alcohol consumption, pill count, and AE
L760: monitoring. Diet records were returned at each of these visits 4-7 and 10. Liver imaging and PFTs were performed at the primary efficacy endpoint.
L761: The safety phase began after the efficacy endpoint at week 26. At safety-phase visits 11 through 15 (weeks 36, 46, 56, 66, and 78 \pm 2 weeks), subjects underwent physical exam, vital signs, ECG, laboratory evaluation, GSRS, recording of alcohol consumption, pill count, and AE monitoring. Diet records were returned at weeks 36 and 78. Liver imaging and PFTs were performed at weeks 56 and 78.
L762: On the recommendation of the DSMB, additional time points only for liver monitoring were implemented in February 2009. In addition to the time points described above, ALT, AST, total bilirubin, and alkaline phosphatase were measured at weeks 31, 41, and 51; therefore, in the final protocol, liver-related laboratory tests were obtained at a minimum of every 4-5 weeks until week 56.
L763: Visit 15 (week 78) was the final visit for subjects who proceeded into the optional HoFH-extension study. Patients who did not enroll into the extension were asked to return for visit 16 (week 84), six weeks after discontinuing study drug. This visit included a physical examination, vital signs, ECG, laboratory evaluation, GSRS, liver imaging, PFTs, and AE monitoring. Subjects who discontinued early were also asked to return for an early-termination visit that included these assessments.
L764: Table 55 summarizes the schedule of selected assessments.
